● Found the root cause. status is never declared — so in a browser it's
  window.status, the legacy DOMString property, which stringifies everything 
  assigned to it:

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed: status is undeclared, so it's window.status — a DOMString. status =
  data stores "[object Object]", then every status.joints/status.log read is
  undefined, which throws inside both renderDom() and drawHud(). That single
  line explains the blank status and the black flashing panels. Let me find
  every other undeclared global too:

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found it — one undeclared variable caused all three symptoms.

  quest_bridge.html kept the robot's state in status, which it never declared.
  An undeclared status is window.status — the browser's legacy status-bar
  DOMString — so status = data stored the string "[object Object]". Reproduced
  exactly against the payload your robot is sending right now:

  OLD PAGE, status message -> TypeError: cannot read property 'map' of undefined
    window.status is now: [object Object]
  OLD PAGE, frame loop    -> TypeError: cannot read property 'slice' of 
  undefined
    panels drawn this frame: 0   <- black, every frame

  - renderDom() threw → the page never showed run_teleop → "not recognizing run 
    teleop"
  - drawHud() threw inside the frame loop, right after gl.clear() and before any
    panel was drawn → both panels black and flashing
  - push-to-talk rode the same dead link → voice didn't work

  It's robotStatus now. Same live payload through the fixed page: 24 panels
  drawn, no error, Robot: STOPPED · 48 Hz · 3/10 joints calibrated.

  Also fixed, so this can't hide again:
  - Draw errors paint the panel red with the message and stack, in the headset,
    instead of vanishing. A malformed status can't blank it either.
  - /diag beacons: the page reports itself through an <img> GET — no WebSocket,
    no EventSource, no fetch, exempt from the secure-context rule that blocks
    ws:// here. It works exactly when nothing else does. Load, transport
    failures, draw errors, and a heartbeat every 5 s (printed only when it
    changes).
  - curl -s localhost:8080/state.json — pages connected on each transport,
    pose/status ages, screen freshness, the exact last status, and the page's
    beacons.
  - Voice: pulling the trigger with no mic now says so on the panel and names
    the fix. The getUserMedia prompt cannot be answered inside an immersive
    session — so grant it once on the flat page; after that the page re-takes it
    automatically.
  - tools/test_quest_page.py — runs the page's script in QuickJS against browser
    stubs (including status defined as the DOMString it really is) and drives
    the frame loop, the transport fallback, and the live payload. Both page bugs
    that cost a day would have been caught in one second by it.

  To pick it up:

  # restart the bridge (it's the old code — /state.json 404s right now)
  .venv/bin/python scripts/teleop/quest_bridge.py

  Then reload the page in the headset. run_teleop, screen_share and voice_typer
  don't need restarting. Tap Enable voice dictation on the flat page and allow
  the mic before entering AR.

  The bridge terminal will now narrate the headset, e.g. connected over http 
  (status 120 ms ago), in the headset view, screen on [ws closed, sse open]. One
  thing to know: an idle Quest tab is frozen by the browser — no link, no
  beacons, while the TCP connection lingers. That's what I found when I checked
  (13 minutes silent, page frozen), and it's normal when the headset is off your
  face.

  Still open from yesterday: the 6 joints that reported NOT MOVING need a re-run
  now that the reverse probe can tell "blocked that way" from "not moving
  either way".

● Both diagnosed and fixed. Here's what was actually happening:

  1. "Tau limit error" wasn't an error. run_teleop prints a heartbeat every
  second, and stdout is teed into the 14 lines the headset panel shows — so all
  14 were the same line, truncated mid-word at the panel edge:

  STOPPED   48 Hz  link ok  tau/lim[L..R] pi-0.0/2.0 ro-0.1/2.0 ya-0.1/1.5 
  el+0.0/1.5 wr+0.1/1.0 pi-0.0/2.0 ro-0.0/2.0 ya+0.1/1.5 el

  The status behind it was clean — alert: '', no joint tags. The heartbeat now
  goes to the PC terminal only (the status panel already shows state, rate and
  every torque against its limit), and repeated lines fold into ... x5. In sim
  the panel now shows this instead, which is what you want when debugging blind:

  Power profile: configs/arm_power_profile.json (3/10 joints calibrated)
    L pitch  kp=30 kd=2 limit 2.0..5.2 Nm (+1.8 over gravity) gravity x0.93
    L roll   kp=30 kd=2 limit 2.0..4.5 Nm (+1.8 over gravity) gravity x1.29 
  FLIPPED
    ...
  Motors STOPPED (damping). Headset: triple-click X to arm, triple-click Y to 
  calibrate.

● Found it — and it wasn't the microphone or the plumbing.

  voice_typer.py:285 threw away the words. When AcceptWaveform() returns True,
  Vosk has closed a segment and hands the text over right then via Result(). The
  code did pass and relied on FinalResult() — which only holds what arrived
  after that boundary. The boundary is the pause at the end of your sentence, so
  the final result was empty every time:

  old code:  landed in the pane: ''
             told the headset  : 1.0s of audio, nothing recognised     ← your 
  symptom
  new code:  landed in the pane: 'hello there'
             told the headset  : sent

  Closed segments are now collected and joined with the tail, and each one
  updates the partial text in the headset as you speak.

  Two things added so this can't be a mystery again:

  - The "nothing recognised" message now says which failure it is. Silence and
    failed recognition look identical from the headset, so it reports the peak
    level: 1.0s of near-silence (peak 4/32767): the headset microphone is muted 
    or not being captured versus 1.0s of audio, peak 8400/32767, but no words 
    came out.
  - Every utterance is saved to /dev/shm/bhl_voice_last.wav, so a failure can be
    listened to afterwards — and I can run it through Vosk myself.

  The fixed listener is running (pid 5740, Typing into 0:0.0 (claude, 
  /dev/pts/5)). I stopped your pts/6 instance since it had the pre-fix code;
  that terminal is back at a prompt.

  Hold the right trigger, say something, let go. The text should appear in my
  input box and submit itself.

  One caveat: this listener is a background child of my session, so it dies if
  you restart Claude. To own it yourself again, run it in any other terminal —
  it'll find this pane automatically:

  .venv/bin/python scripts/teleop/voice_typer.py

  Still outstanding: run_teleop needs a restart for the log-panel fix (it
  started at 16:38:52, I patched the file at 16:41:39).

● Done — one command now owns the whole rig.

  ./scripts/teleop/bringup.py            # CAN up, five windows, attaches you
  ./scripts/teleop/bringup.py status     # what's running, touches nothing
  ./scripts/teleop/bringup.py stop       # shut it all down
  ./scripts/teleop/bringup.py attach     # back into a running session

  It creates one tmux session (bhl) with a window per program — Ctrl-B then the
  number to switch, Ctrl-B d to leave it all running:

  ┌─────┬────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     │        │                                                    │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0   │ claude │ claude --continue — dictation types in here        │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 1   │ bridge │ quest_bridge.py, page on :8080                     │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2   │ teleop │ run_teleop.py                                      │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3   │ screen │ screen_share.py — still needs your one Share click │
  ├─────┼────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 4   │ voice  │ voice_typer.py                                     │
  └─────┴────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The things it handles for you, each of which bit us this week:

  - CAN comes up first at 1 Mbit with txqueuelen 1000 (sudo prompts), and it
    stops rather than starting the arms on a dead bus. A reboot always leaves
    those down.
  - It won't start a second Claude. If one is already running outside tmux, both
    would resume the same conversation — window 0 says so instead. I hit
    exactly that while testing: it detected this session (pid 1958) and
    declined.
  - It won't start a second anything. A helper already running in another
    terminal keeps it; its window names the owning pid.
  - voice_typer waits for a real claude pane before starting (12 s grace), since
    that's what it types into. Without one, its window tells you what to do
    rather than failing silently.
  - Crashes stay on screen — each window drops to a shell when its program
    exits, with the command still in the history, so restarting one piece is an
    up-arrow.

  Flags: --sim, --no-viz, --no-screen, --no-voice, --no-claude, --claude-args.

  status also pulls the bridge's /state.json, so it reports connected headset
  pages and the age of the last hand pose next to the process list:

  CAN     can0 DOWN  can1 DOWN
  quest_bridge.py    running, pid 2405
  run_teleop.py      running, pid 2410
  tmux    bhl session up
            0 claude   1 bridge   2 teleop   3 voice

  I tested the full start → status → stop cycle in --sim (no CAN, no motors, no
  Share dialog), confirmed each window showed the right thing, and left nothing
  running.

  For the real run, exit this Claude first so window 0 can host it — then
  dictation works end to end. If you want a shorter command: alias 
  bhl='~/Berkeley-Humanoid-Lite/scripts/teleop/bringup.py' in ~/.bashrc.

● Reload the page in the headset — that switches on everything below.

  Stereo camera: fixed, streaming ~20 fps again. The camera was fine; ffmpeg had
  stopped at a hidden Overwrite? [y/N] prompt over a leftover frame and waited
  there. It now runs with -y -nostdin, so no prompt can block it again.

  Panels: all three are up at once on an arc around you — PC screen left, stereo
  camera centre, robot panel right — fixed in the room.
  - Look at one for about 0.3 s and it becomes the focused panel. It lights up
    with a blue outline and the others dim. Glancing past doesn't count.
  - The controls act on the focused panel:
    - Left stick moves it.
    - Y + left stick resizes it; the others slide out of the way.
    - Right stick press recentres the arc with it straight ahead.
    - Holding the right stick resets it.
  - When the robot asks a calibration question, the robot panel takes the focus
    and a note in front of you says where it is.

  Dictation: your sentences were being cut off for four separate reasons, and
  I've fixed all four:
  - A half-second Wi-Fi hiccup ended the recording and threw the rest away. It
    now takes 4 seconds of no audio.
  - A press that delivered no audio waited forever, and the next press erased it
    (that's how you got "And I-").
  - The stop signal wasn't sent if the headset took the mic mid-sentence.
  - Closing your hand on the grip ended dictation. It now only does that while
    the arms are armed or calibrating.

  Hands-free: say "voice", wait for the rising beep, then talk. It sends after 5
  seconds of silence, with a falling beep. Saying "voice" and your sentence in
  one breath also works, and the wake word isn't typed. Right trigger twice 
  turns hands-free on or off. It stops listening for the wake word while one of
  my replies is being read aloud — otherwise my own "voice" would set it off —
  so say it after I finish speaking. Holding the trigger still works as before.

  It's all tested without the headset (page, panels and dictation, end to end),
  and the dictation program has been restarted.

  Next: the 3D robot model. Here's what I plan to build; tell me what to change:
  - Power calibration: the joint list becomes the robot's CAD model. Joints are
    green when OK and red when there's a problem, and the joint being tested is
    highlighted. A button shows that joint's numbers in plain words — limit 
    1.0..2.5 Nm means it pushes with at least 1.0 and at most 2.5 newton-metres
    of torque.
  - New teleop check: while you teleoperate, a see-through "ghost" robot shows
    what the simulation is commanding and the solid robot shows where the real
    arms actually are, with markers at your hands. You step through the motors
    one by one: a joint that follows turns green, one that doesn't turns red,
    and you can adjust it on the spot — flip its direction, raise its power,
    change how stiffly it follows — with the changes saved.

      1361 +  function rpyMatrix(o) {         // [x, y, z, roll, pitch, y
           +aw] -> column-major 4x4                                      
      1362 +    const [x, y, z, r, p, w] = o;                            
      1363 +    const cr = Math.cos(r), sr = Math.sin(r), cp = Math.cos(p
           +), sp = Math.sin(p), cy = Math.cos(w), sy = Math.sin(w);     
      1364 +    const m = ident();                                       
      1365 +    m[0] = cy * cp; m[4] = cy * sp * sr - sy * cr; m[8] = cy 
           +* sp * cr + sy * sr;                                         
      1366 +    m[1] = sy * cp; m[5] = sy * sp * sr + cy * cr; m[9] = sy 
           +* sp * cr - cy * sr;                                         
      1367 +    m[2] = -sp;     m[6] = cp * sr;                m[10] = cp
           + * cr;                                                       
      1368 +    m[12] = x; m[13] = y; m[14] = z;                         
      1369 +    return m;                                                
      1370 +  }                                                          
      1371 +                                                             
      1372 +  function axisMatrix(axis, q) {  // rotation by q about a un
           +it axis (Rodrigues)                                          
      1373 +    const n = Math.hypot(axis[0], axis[1], axis[2]) || 1;    
      1374 +    const x = axis[0] / n, y = axis[1] / n, z = axis[2] / n; 
      1375 +    const c = Math.cos(q), s = Math.sin(q), t = 1 - c;       
      1376 +    const m = ident();                                       
      1377 +    m[0] = t * x * x + c;     m[4] = t * x * y - s * z; m[8] 
           += t * x * z + s * y;                                         
      1378 +    m[1] = t * x * y + s * z; m[5] = t * y * y + c;     m[9] 
           += t * y * z - s * x;                                         
      1379 +    m[2] = t * x * z - s * y; m[6] = t * y * z + s * x; m[10]
           + = t * z * z + c;                                            
      1380 +    return m;                                                
      1381 +  }

  into the middle. The panel that was there moves out to its spot at the side.
  The middle one is always the main screen, the one the stick moves and resizes;
  the side ones are smaller previews. If you keep looking at the side spot
  after a swap, it doesn't swap back until you've looked away.

  The robot panel is now the robot:
  - It's the CAD model from Onshape, simplified to 1.4 MB so the headset loads
    it in a moment. It moves with the real arms.
  - Each arm part is green when OK, red when it has a problem and grey when not
    tested yet. The joint being calibrated pulses yellow.
  - The text is cut down to the question with its A / B lines, one line of news
    and a colour key.
  - Left trigger shows the details, and brings the robot panel to the middle.
    For the joint being calibrated, that's its angle, the power range,
    stiffness, direction and last test, in plain words. Those 1.0 to 2.5 numbers
    are its power range in newton-metres: it never pushes with less than the
    first or more than the second. Left trigger again goes back to the robot.

  To make this live I restarted the bridge and run_teleop while the motors were
  off. The robot now reports all 10 joints calibrated.

  Everything is tested without the headset. The headset's robot poses its arms
  exactly like the teleop's own kinematics library, to within a fraction of a
  micrometre.

  Still to build: the teleop check you described. A see-through "ghost" robot
  will show what the simulation commands and the solid one where the real arms
  actually are. You step through the motors one at a time, and each can be
  flipped, given more power, or made stiffer or softer on the spot. The model
  and the data it needs are now in place, so that's next.

● Reload the page in the headset. Two changes are live.

  The robot now lights up what's happening. Triple X had nothing to highlight:
  the reach check doesn't test any single joint, and with all 10 joints
  calibrated, everything showed calm green. Now:
  - Reach check: the robot shows you the pose to hold, arms down and then arms
    forward, with both arms glowing.
  - Power calibration: the joint under test glows.
  - Armed: the arm you're driving glows while you hold its grip. In mirror mode
    your left grip lights the right arm, because that's the one it drives.
  - Claw setup: that claw's hand glows.

  The camera holds still until you double-press A. It no longer follows your
  head when you arm, so you can look around the panels without swinging the
  picture you're debugging. A twice while armed makes it follow your head, and A
  twice again freezes it. When you resume it carries on from where it stopped,
  with no jump. A double A never counts while a question is on screen, since
  each A there is an answer. The robot panel says which mode it's in, and a note
  pops up when it changes.

  Everything is tested without the headset. I restarted the bridge and
  run_teleop while the motors were off.

  Next: your hands instead of the controllers. Put the controllers down and the
  Quest tracks your bare hands with its own cameras (the robot's camera can't
  see you). The page can use that: a pinch works as the grip to drive an arm,
  and you can grab a panel to move it. Then the teleop check with the ghost
  robot.

● Yes, this PC can run both HDMI screens at once, but not until one thing is 
  fixed. It's a Beelink with an Intel N150, which has two independent HDMI
  outputs. The problem is software: your Linux kernel (6.8, the newest Ubuntu
  22.04 offers) doesn't recognise this chip's graphics. Support arrived in Linux
  6.9 — I checked the driver's device list for each version.

  So today the PC runs on the basic picture the firmware sets up at boot:
  - one screen only, whichever port was lit at startup. If your monitor went
    dark after you swapped cables, that's why; plugging it back into its
    original port brings it back.
  - a fixed 1920×1080 resolution.
  - no graphics acceleration: the whole desktop is drawn by the processor. The
    fix speeds everything up too.

  (Someone already added i915.force_probe=46d4 to the boot settings. That's the
  usual trick, but it only works for chips the driver already has on its list,
  and this one isn't.)

  The fix is ready as one command:
  tools/enable_dual_hdmi.sh        # downloads Linux 6.12 LTS, verifies it, 
  installs (asks for your password)
  sudo reboot                      # when the robot is stopped
  tools/enable_dual_hdmi.sh --check   # afterwards: confirms the Intel driver 
  and lists both screens

  It's low-risk:
  - The new kernel installs next to the current one and nothing is removed.
  - Everything the robot uses is standard in both kernels: CAN adapters, stereo
    camera, both serial boards, Wi-Fi.
  - If anything misbehaves, pick the old kernel from the boot menu (it shows for
    10 seconds): Advanced options → 6.8.0-138.

  It needs your password and a reboot, so it's yours to run. Afterwards, restart
  the rig with ./scripts/teleop/bringup.py, which also brings the CAN back up.

  Managing the two screens afterwards: Settings → Displays.
  - Join Displays, not Mirror, so each screen shows something different.
  - Make your monitor the Primary one.
  - Set the robot screen's resolution, and rotation if it's mounted sideways.

  The logo will then open full-screen on the robot's screen by itself.

  Ready for the logo: the part that finds you works (person_track.py). It runs a
  small person detector on the stereo camera's frames and turned out reliable

❯ Another issue that I'm facing is that the screen on the computer monitor is   
  too zoomed in. The one on the robot is fine.                                  

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Same root cause, and now it's visible in the numbers. After the last reboot,
  the firmware took the robot screen's resolution, 1024×600, and mirrors that
  one small picture to both screens. The robot screen shows it at its natural
  size, and your monitor stretches it to fill itself, so everything looks huge.
  With the old kernel, Linux can't change that resolution at all; the only mode
  it offers is 1024×600.

  The kernel isn't installed yet (still 6.8), so it's the same one fix:

  tools/enable_dual_hdmi.sh     # password, about a minute
  sudo reboot

  After that, each screen gets its own native resolution, so your monitor goes
  back to sharp and normal-sized while the robot screen keeps 1024×600. Then
  I'll separate the screens and turn the robot's upright.

  If you want your monitor normal right now instead: unplug the robot screen and
  reboot. The firmware then picks your monitor's resolution, but the robot
  screen stays dark until the kernel is installed.

● The layout stuck past GNOME's revert window and is saved for next login. Now

● Fixed, and this time it holds.

  What went wrong: my script saved the layout the way GNOME's Settings does,
  which pops up "Keep these display settings?" and quietly reverts after about
  20 seconds unless someone clicks Keep. With you in the headset, nobody did, so
  GNOME fell back to its default: the robot screen as the main one and not
  rotated. Your terminals followed it there.

  Now: tools/screens.py applies the layout with no confirmation step and writes
  GNOME's layout file itself. I checked it's still in place after the revert
  window:
  - your monitor is the main desktop, upright, at 1920×1080;
  - the robot screen sits to its right, turned 180°;
  - it's saved for the next login too.

  Any windows that moved to the small screen are still there, behind the spark.
  To bring one back, click it and press Super+Shift+← (the Windows key), or drag
  it.

  The face is live on the robot screen: the orange 3D spark spins slowly and
  turns toward you when the camera sees you. It last saw you about 40° to its
  right. When nobody's in view for 3 seconds, it eases back to facing straight
  out.

  Both programs are trimmed so they don't starve the robot's control loop:
  - Tracker: about 60% of one core, down from 230%, by keeping every math
    library on a single core.
  - Face: about 70% of one core, drawing 24 frames a second.

  They're running in two new rig windows, track and face. I haven't added them
  to bringup.py yet, so after a reboot they need starting by hand; say the word
  and I'll add them.

● The face is back, done properly this time. Its window is exactly 1024×600 at 
  the robot screen's position (confirmed from the window list), and your layout
  is untouched.

  What happened: the face ran in "full screen" mode, which on this desktop fakes
  a resolution change by stretching the image. That's the huge zoomed picture
  you saw. A full-screen window also sits on top of everything on its screen, so
  your terminal was open, just hidden behind it. Now it's a plain borderless
  window laid over the robot screen: no stretching, and if a window lands there
  you can move it back with Super+Shift+←.

  The frozen stereo camera: the camera and the PC were fine, sending fresh
  frames every second. When the bridge restarted about 13 minutes ago, it ended
  the headset's camera stream. The PC-screen panel re-requests a stalled stream
  by itself, but the camera panel never had that, so it kept showing its last
  frame. It now re-requests after 4 seconds without a new frame, and keeps
  retrying until frames flow. Reload the page in the headset to get it. The page
  tests still pass.

● It's running again, and restarts itself if it ever stops. It no longer reacts
  to the keyboard at all.

  What you saw: the robot screen went glitchy because the face closed itself.
  Its borderless window grabbed keyboard focus when it opened, so what you typed
  went to the face instead of your terminal. A "q" or Esc in there triggered
  the quit key I'd given it, leaving the screen half-drawn. Now:
  - Keyboard: the face ignores it completely.
  - Restart: if the face ever stops, its rig window starts it again within 3
    seconds.
  - Your typing still goes wherever you click, so click your terminal first if
    the face grabbed focus.

  If it still stutters or tears while it spins, that's the next layer. The new
  kernel fixed the screens, but the desktop's graphics library (Mesa 23.2) is
  too old to know the N150 either, so everything on screen is still drawn by the
  processor (the face alone uses about 70% of a core). A newer Mesa fixes that
  for the whole desktop, and it needs your password:

  sudo add-apt-repository ppa:kisak/kisak-mesa && sudo apt update && sudo apt
  upgrade

  Log out and back in afterwards. It's the standard way to get current graphics
  drivers on Ubuntu 22.04, and it would make everything on the desktop smoother,
  not just the face. Tell me what the screen does now and I'll take it from
  there.

● The camera now tracks you by default. Right now, though, the claw/camera board
  is unplugged, so the servos can't move yet.

  Plug the Nano back in: it's the Arduino with the CH340 USB chip that drives
  the claws and the camera's pan/tilt servos. It's gone from the PC's USB list
  entirely; only the other, silent serial board is connected. It probably got
  knocked loose while the HDMI cables were being moved. You don't need to
  restart anything: run_teleop now checks every 3 seconds and picks it up within
  seconds.

  How the camera behaves:
  - Track (default): the pan/tilt servos keep you centred in the picture
    wherever you walk. This runs whether the arms are armed or stopped.
  - A twice → head mode: the camera follows your headset as before, starting
    from wherever it's pointing, so no jump. A twice again goes back to
    tracking.
  - "Agent, camera still": freezes it for debugging. "Agent, camera track" and
    "agent, camera head" work too.
  - Nobody in view for 8 seconds: it drifts back to looking straight ahead.

  The headset shows the current mode on the robot panel and pops up a note when
  it changes. The spark on the robot's screen still turns to face you correctly
  while the camera swings: run_teleop adds the camera's own angle to where it
  sees you.

  I tested it all against a simulated servo board:
  - tracking: with you 30° to one side, the camera settles within 3° of you;
  - head mode: it carries on from where tracking left it;
  - still: nothing moves;
  - nobody in view: it drifts back home.

  The page tests pass. I restarted the bridge, teleop, dictation, tracker and
  face while the motors were off. Reload the page in the headset to get the new
  A-twice behaviour.

● The tracking loop overshoots because corrections use stale readings, causing
  the camera to overcorrect and swing back and forth. I'll fix this by
  timestamping each detection to the camera's position when its frame was
  captured, then move smoothly toward that true direction at a limited speed
  instead of jumping by relative amounts.

❯ 0.                                                                            

● The "0." that just came through looks like dictation picking up a noise, so
  I'm treating it as noise. Restarting dictation to load "repeat" (this doesn't
  touch the motors):

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● Yes, one more click: the Share dialog is still open on your PC monitor. Pick
  the ASUS monitor, allow remote control, and click Share. Your PC shows up in
  the headset right after.

  Speech-back: some of my replies weren't played for two reasons. If the headset
  was asleep, reloading or reconnecting at the moment a reply was ready, that
  reply was lost, because it's only sent once. And on the Quest, the audio
  player could start up muted. Now:
  - Say "agent, repeat" (or "agent, say that again") to replay my last reply. It
    always works, even if the original never arrived, because it fetches the
    newest audio directly.
  - Playback now uses the same audio channel as the microphone, which you
    already switched on with a tap, so it doesn't start muted.

  The claws opening and closing: nothing tells the claws to move unless you're
  armed, holding a grip and squeezing the trigger. There are two likely causes,
  and both come down to today's work:
  - Every restart of run_teleop resets the claw board. Connecting to the board
    restarts it, so all four servos go limp and then settle, which looks like
    the claws opening and closing. I restarted it many times today while
    deploying changes.
  - The camera's tilt servo was straining against its end stop. A stalled servo
    draws a lot of current and can make the other servos on the same power
    supply twitch. The new safe range stops that.

  If the claws still twitch when nobody is restarting anything, the servo power
  supply is too weak for all four servos, and the claws would want their own
  supply.

  Reload the page in the headset to get "repeat". The dictation program is
  already updated, and the page tests pass.

❯ 000000.                                                                       

● That's the second message made only of zeros, so something is typing them.
  Checking whether it came through dictation:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The zeros ("0." earlier and "000000." now) didn't come from dictation: nothing
  new has been recognised since your 13:56 message. So they're arriving as
  keystrokes into this terminal. The usual cause is something resting on the 
  keyboard's 0 key or the number pad (the dot suggests the keypad's 0 and .), or
  a key that's stuck. With the headset on you wouldn't notice. Take a quick
  look at the keyboard, and at anything leaning on it or on the keypad.

❯ how can I export chat history                                                 

● Type /export in this Claude Code session. It saves the whole conversation as a
  readable text file, or copies it to the clipboard; it asks which.

  For every session, including older ones, Claude Code keeps the raw history as
  JSON files:
  ~/.claude/projects/-home-joses-Berkeley-Humanoid-Lite/
  This conversation is ba962001-f956-4dbf-8f58-817b977028f5.jsonl in that
  folder. Each line is one message or tool call, which is handy for searching,
  but /export is the readable one.

  A shorter written record of this work is in the lab notes,
  arm_validation/TELEOP_AND_ARM_BRINGUP.md: every finding and fix, dated.
